## Page 1


The Parameterised Complexity of Temporal Motif
Counting, and a Lov´asz-Style Isomorphism Theorem
Jayakrishnan Madathil #
Indian Institute of Technology Palakkad
Kitty Meeks #
School of Computing Science, University of Glasgow
Marc Roth #
School of Electronic Engineering and Computer Science, Queen Mary University of London
Abstract
We study the structural expressivity and the parameterised complexity of counting homomorphisms from small
temporal patterns to large temporal graphs. Here, a temporal pattern 𝑃consists of a graph together with a partial
order on its edges, and a homomorphism from 𝑃to a temporal graph must not only preserve edges, but also satisfy
the temporal constraints imposed by the partial order of the edge set of the pattern.
The main results of this work are three-fold:
(1) We prove a temporal Lov´asz-style theorem, stating that two temporal graphs are isomorphic (under a natural
definition of temporal isomorphisms) if and only if they have the same number of homomorphisms from all
temporal patterns.
(2) We introduce a cliquewidth-based measure on temporal patterns, called the temporally order-augmented dual
width, the toadwidth for short, and show that counting temporal homomorphisms is fixed-parameter tractable for
temporal patterns of bounded toadwidth.
(3) We provide a parameterised complexity dichotomy with an explicit tractability criterion for counting homomor-
phisms from totally ordered temporal patterns, classified along their underlying graph structure.
The methods and tools invoked for proving (1)-(3) vary significantly: The proof of the Lov´asz-style Theorem is
obtained by combining Lov´asz’ original argument with an inclusion-exclusion construction to deal with temporal
equalities and inequalities. The FPT algorithm in (2) is obtained by an involved dynamic programming algorithm
along toadwidth. Finally, the upper bound for the dichotomy in (3) relies on the toadwidth-based algorithm in (2),
and the lower bound follows from a reduction from the clique problem by embedding the temporal pattern into a
grid, the connections of which are either formed by edges or by temporal constraints.

J. Madathil, K. Meeks, and M. Roth

Introduction
Temporal graphs model networks the connections of which are only available at certain points, or
during certain intervals, of time. They find applications in the analysis of protein-protein interaction
networks [23], social networks [41], and in machine learning [31, 40], only to name a few examples; we
refer the reader to the survey of Holme and Saram¨aki [26] for a comprehensive exposition.
Following the success of “Network Motifs” in static (non-temporal) graphs [34] recent years have seen
a flurry of applied results on temporal motif counting problems [36, 30, 39, 29]: in a nutshell, given a pattern
𝑃and a temporal graph Γ, the task is to count the number of occurrences of 𝑃in Γ. Similarly as in the
static case, it has been observed that frequencies of temporal patterns correlate with global features of the
temporal network. Despite their relevance in practical applications witnessed by the previous works, we
find a notable gap when it comes to our theoretical understanding on the inherent complexity of temporal
motif counting problems: while there are results for selected patterns, such as temporal walks [17], and
temporal stars [29], the state of the art is far away from a comprehensive understanding of the complexity
of counting temporal patterns in general. This stands in sharp contrast to the static, non-temporal, case,
for which we know deep dichotomy results w.r.t. parameterised and fine-grained complexity theory that
determine almost precisely the best possible running times for arbitrary motif counting problems under
standard lower bound assumptions [13, 33, 11, 10, 20, 15].
In this work, we address this gap and present the first comprehensive complexity analysis of temporal
motif counting problems.
The Model: Temporal Graphs and Parameterised Complexity
The literature exhibits a variety of different, but equivalent, ways to define temporal graphs: for example,
the snapshot model [31, 21] introduces a temporal graph as a sequence of static graphs on the same set of
vertices, where the 𝑖-th element of the sequence represents the edges present at time 𝑖. The time-varying
model [4] equips a graph 𝐺with a function 𝜌: 𝐸(𝐺) × 𝒯→{0, 1}, where 𝒯is the set of available times,
and 𝜌(𝑒, 𝑖) = 1 if and only if 𝑒is available at time 𝑖. Note that the latter model accounts for both discrete
time (𝒯⊆N) and continuous time (𝒯= R≥0). In the present work we use the discrete time-varying
model, defined as follows:
Definition 1.1 (Temporal Graphs). A temporal graph is a pair Γ = (𝐺, 𝜏) of a graph 𝐺without selfloops,
but with parallel edges allowed, and a mapping 𝜏: 𝐸(𝐺) →N satisfying that 𝜏(𝑒) ≠𝜏(𝑒′) for each pair of
distinct parallel edges 𝑒, 𝑒′ ∈𝐸(𝐺).
Note that the previous definition is equivalent to the standard definition of Kempe, Kleinberg, and
Kumar [28] by enforcing 𝐺to be a graph without parallel edges, but allowing 𝜏to map edges to finite
subsets of N. The latter is also equivalent to the aforementioned time-varying model by setting 𝜌(𝑒, 𝑖) = 1
if and only if 𝑖∈𝜏(𝑒). As those encodings can trivially be transformed into each other, our complexity-
theoretic results are invariant on the choice; we emphasise that we use the specific model in Definition 1.1
solely due to the fact that it will be most convenient, notation-wise, for our proofs.
To study the complexity of temporal motif counting we rely on the framework of parameterised
complexity theory: Given a pattern 𝑃and a temporal graph (𝐺, 𝜏) for which we are interested in the
number of ocurences of 𝑃, we assume that 𝑃is significantly smaller than 𝐺. This assumption is clearly
reflected by existing practical applications of temporal motif counting, and captures, precisely, the
intuition behind temporal network motifs being small and simple patterns whose frequencies in large

Motif Counting in Temporal Graphs
networks correlate with global features. We also note that the same assumption is standard for the case of
static (non-temporal) motif counting [34, 10].
In the language of parameterised complexity, we say that a temporal motif counting problem is
fixed-parameter tractable (FPT) if there is a computable function 𝑓such that that problem can be solved in
time 𝑓(|𝑃|) · |(𝐺, 𝜏)|𝑂(1), where |𝑃| is the size of the pattern, and |(𝐺, 𝜏)| is the size of the temporal graph.
Temporal Patterns and Homomorphisms
So far, we have not provided details on what is meant, formally, by a temporal pattern or an occurrence of
a pattern in a temporal graph. Before introducing the mathematical details, let us consider a well-studied
example: temporal walks and paths [28, 2, 5, 17]. A temporal walk of size 𝑘in a temporal graph (𝐺, 𝜏)
is a walk in 𝐺consisting of 𝑘edges 𝑒1, . . . , 𝑒𝑘such that 𝜏(𝑒1) ≤𝜏(𝑒2) ≤· · · ≤𝜏(𝑒𝑘). Similarly, a temporal
path of size 𝑘is a self-avoiding temporal walk of size 𝑘. A temporal walk can be equivalently expressed
as a homomorphism1 from 𝑃𝑘to 𝐺such that, for each 𝑖∈[𝑘−1] the time step of the image of the 𝑖’th
edge is at most the time step of the image of the (𝑖+ 1)’st edge. Moreover, a temporal path can be defined
similarly via subgraph embeddings, that is, vertex-injective homomorphisms.
In other words, the previous examples highlight that we can model temporal patterns as a combination
of static patterns, such as homomorphisms and subgraph embeddings, and temporal constraints.
Given that graph homomorphisms counts have shown to be, literally, the basis of static network motif
counting [10]2, we will model temporal motif counting via temporal homomorphisms in the present paper:
Definition 1.2 (Temporal Patterns and Homomorphisms). A temporal pattern is a triple 𝑃= (𝐻, 𝑅, 𝜗)
where 𝐻is a (static) graph without parallel edges, 𝑅is a finite poset, and 𝜗is a surjection from 𝐸(𝐻) to
the groundset of 𝑅such that for every distinct pair 𝑒, 𝑒′ of parallel edges of 𝐻, we have 𝜗(𝑒) ≠𝜗(𝑒′).
Given a temporal graph Γ = (𝐺, 𝜏), a homomorphism from 𝑃to Γ is a pair 𝜑= (𝜈, 𝜉) such that
(1) 𝜈: 𝑉(𝐻) →𝑉(𝐺),
(2) 𝜉: 𝐸(𝐻) →𝐸(𝐺) and for all 𝑢, 𝑣∈𝑉(𝐻) and edge 𝑒∈𝐸(𝐻) with endpoints 𝑢and 𝑣, we have that 𝜈(𝑢)
and 𝜈(𝑣) are the endpoints of 𝜉(𝑒),
(3) for all 𝑒, 𝑒′ ∈𝐸(𝐻) we have that
𝜗(𝑒) ≤𝑅𝜗(𝑒′) ⇒𝜏(𝜉(𝑒)) ≤𝜏(𝜉(𝑒′))  implying that 𝜗(𝑒) = 𝜗(𝑒′) ⇒𝜏(𝜉(𝑒)) = 𝜏(𝜉(𝑒′))
.
We write Hom(𝑃→Γ) for the set of all homomorphisms from 𝑃to Γ
We note that, in the previous definition, (1) and (2) enforce (𝜈, 𝜉) to be a graph homomorphism from 𝐻to
𝐺, and (3) enforces that the homomorphism satisfies the temporal constraints imposed by the poset 𝑅.
Specifically, we highlight that the condition 𝜗(𝑒) = 𝜗(𝑒′) ⇒𝜏(𝜉(𝑒)) = 𝜏(𝜉(𝑒′)) allows us to enforce that
distinct edges of the pattern are mapped to edges of the temporal graph that are present at the same time —
this illustrates the reason on why we do not just enforce 𝑅to be a poset on 𝐸(𝐻), but rather to allow 𝑅
to be any finite poset the ground set of which is the image of the surjection 𝜗. Examples of temporal
patterns are depicted in Figures 1 and 3.
1 A homomorphism from a graph 𝐻to a graph 𝐺is an adjacency preserving mapping from 𝑉(𝐻) to 𝑉(𝐺); see Section 2 for
the formal definition.
2 See also [38, Section 4] for a survey on the homomorphism basis.

Remark 1.3 (Temporal Patterns vs. Temporal Graphs). We wish to highlight explicitly the fact that
temporal graphs have edges with specific time steps while temporal patterns have relative constraints
along pairs of edges via the surjection into a poset. This is due to the fact that we wish to express that
edges in the image of a homomorphism satisfy temporal constraints such as “no later than” or “at the
same time”, rather than enforcing that the image of an edge needs to happen at a specific time; think for
example of temporal paths and temporal walks which require that the times of the edges of the paths and
walks are monotonically increasing.
1.1
Our Results
This work establishes three main contributions:
(1) We show that our choice for modeling temporal motif counting via temporal homomorphisms is not
just some generalisation of counting temporal walks, but captures the principal expressivity desired
and expected from a foundation for temporal motif counting: we establish a Lov´asz-style theorem
stating that two temporal graphs are isomorphic (under a standard notion of temporal isomorphisms)
if, and only if, they have the same number of temporal homomorphisms from all temporal patterns. In
other words, the homomorphism counts from temporal patterns determine precisely the isomorphism
type of a temporal graph.
(2) We introduce a novel cliquewidth-based invariant, called the temporally order-augmented dual width
(“toadwidth”) on temporal patterns that does take into account not only the graph underlying the
temporal pattern, but also its temporal constraints. We then provide an FPT algorithm for counting
homomorphisms from temporal patterns of bounded toadwidth.
(3) We show that our FPT result is optimal for totally ordered temporal patterns if we restrict input
instances only by the underlying static graph of the temporal patterns. More specifically, we establish
an explicit criterion on classes of (static) graphs ℋwhich, if satisfied, makes the problem of counting
temporal homomorphisms from totally ordered patterns with underlying graphs in ℋfixed-parameter
tractable, and which, if not satisfied, makes the problem hard for the parameterised complexity
class W[1].
In what follows, we present each contribution in detail.
A Temporal Lov´asz Theorem
One of Lov´asz’s most famous results, often referred to as “Lov´asz’s Theorem” states that the isomorphism
type of a graph 𝐺is determined by both infinite vectors (#Hom(𝐻→𝐺))𝐻and (#Hom(𝐺→𝐻))𝐻, where
#Hom(𝐻→𝐺) denotes the number of graph homomorphisms from 𝐻to 𝐺. For this work, the “left-hand
side” result involving the vector (#Hom(𝐻→𝐺))𝐻plays a more important role, and we can state the
corresponding version of Lov´asz’s Theorem formally as follows
Theorem 1.4 (Lov´asz’s Theorem; cf. Chapter 5.4 in [32]). Two graphs 𝐺1 and 𝐺2 are isomorphic if and only
if #Hom(𝐻→𝐺1) = #Hom(𝐻→𝐺2) for each graph 𝐻.
The above version of Lov´asz’s Theorem, while foundational on its own right, has witnessed a renaissance
over the last decade in the context of the theory of homomorphism indistinguishability which has turned
out to be a bridge between motif counting, descriptive complexity theory, graph neural networks, and
isomorphism heuristics [16, 14, 35].

homomorphisms from such patterns is fixed-parameter tractable. Note that for both patterns, the dashed arrows only
depict the Hasse diagram of the partial order; the full partial order is obtained by taking the transitive closure.
We establish a temporal version of Lov´asz’s Theorem. For the statement however, we have to first
discuss the notion of an isomorphism between temporal graphs. The literature contains various proposals,
featured below. For what follows, given a temporal graph Γ = (𝐺, 𝜏), we write 𝐺𝑖for the subgraph of 𝐺
only containing edges present at time 𝑖.

isomorphic for each 𝑖.

The authors of [24] introduce a third version of temporal isomorphism that interpolates between the
former two and that is motivated by the preservation of temporal paths.3
We claim that, in the context of the current work and temporal motif counting, the first version, i.e.,
pointwise isomorphism, is too weak as it allows to change the vertex identification of the isomorphism
at any timestep, which will not be invariant for connected temporal patterns that enforce multiple time
steps. We furthermore claim that the second version, i.e., timewise isomorphism, is too strong as it would
distinguish e.g. the temporal graphs shown in Figure 2. Finally, the third version provided in [24], while
enabling invariance under the number of homomorphisms from temporal walks, fails to capture global
consistency of the order of the time steps. For the purpose of this work we therefore propose a natural
notion of temporal isomorphisms between Γ1 = (𝐺, 𝜏𝐺) and Γ2 = (𝐻, 𝜏𝐻) as global mappings from 𝑉(𝐺)
to 𝑉(𝐻) that preserve the order of the time steps, which we call order-isomorphisms; an example is provided
in Figure 2.
3 The details of the definition of temporal isomorphisms in [24] are not important for the current paper.

Definition 1.5 (Order-Isomorphisms between temporal graphs). Let Γ1 = (𝐺1, 𝜏1) and Γ2 = (𝐺2, 𝜏2) be
temporal graphs. An order-isomorphism from Γ1 to Γ2 is a pair (𝜋, 𝑡) of an isomorphism4 𝜋= (𝜋𝜈, 𝜋𝜉) from
𝐺1 to 𝐺2 and a bijection 𝑡: 𝜏1(𝐸(𝐺1)) →𝜏2(𝐸(𝐺2)) such that
(1) for all 𝑒∈𝐸(𝐺1) we have 𝑡(𝜏1(𝑒)) = 𝜏2(𝜋𝜉(𝑒)), and
(2) 𝑡is strongly monotonically increasing, i.e., 𝑥< 𝑦implies 𝑡(𝑥) < 𝑡(𝑦).
We write Γ1 o Γ2 if Γ1 and Γ2 are order-isomorphic.
To improve intuition, we provide an alternative way to define order-isomorphisms via normal forms
of temporal graphs: A temporal graph Γ = (𝐺, 𝜏) is in ordered normal form (“ONF”) if 𝜏(𝐸(𝐺)) = [𝑘]
for some 𝑘∈N, that is, the edges of 𝐺contain precisely 𝑘distinct time steps, and there is at least one
edge per time step. This allows us to encode Γ in a snap-shot representation Γ = (𝐺1, . . . , 𝐺𝑘) where
𝑉(𝐺𝑖) = 𝑉(𝐺) for each 𝑖∈[𝑘], and 𝐺𝑖is, as before, the subgraph of 𝐺containing only edges 𝑒with
𝜏(𝑒) = 𝑖; note that, specifically, none of the 𝐺𝑖will have parallel edges as we enforced that parallel
edges have different times in the definition of temporal graphs. We observe that each temporal graph
Γ = (𝐺, 𝜏) with 𝜏(𝐸(𝐺)) = {𝑡1, . . . , 𝑡𝑘} and 𝑡1 < · · · < 𝑡𝑘can be transformed into an ordered normal form by
reassigning times 𝑡𝑖↦→𝑖. In that way, it is easy to see that two temporal graphs Γ = (𝐺, 𝜏) and Γ′ = (𝐺′, 𝜏′)
with normal forms (𝐺1, . . . , 𝐺𝑘) and (𝐺′

We are now able to present our first main result:
Theorem 1.6 (A Temporal Lov´asz’s Theorem). Two temporal graphs Γ1 and Γ2 are order-isomorphic if and
only if for all temporal patterns 𝑃we have #Hom(𝑃→Γ1) = #Hom(𝑃→Γ2).

between static graphs with parallel edges.

Motif Counting in Temporal Graphs
An FPT Algorithm for Temporal Patterns of Bounded Toadwidth
In the second part of this work, we develop an FPT algorithm for counting homomorphisms from temporal
patterns of bounded toadwidth. We start by introducing the latter, which requires us to consider graphs
containing both directed and undirected edges.
Definition 1.7 (Mixed Graph). A mixed graph is a graph that may contain both undirected and directed
edges. For convenience, we call the undirected edges simply ”edges” and the directed edges ”arcs.” For a
mixed graph 𝑀, we use 𝐸(𝑀) to denote the set of edges of 𝑀, and 𝐴(𝑀) to denote the set of arcs of 𝑀.
For vertices 𝑢, 𝑣∈𝑉(𝑀), we denote the undirected edge between 𝑢and 𝑣by 𝑢𝑣or {𝑢, 𝑣}, and we denote
the arc directed from 𝑢to 𝑣by (𝑢, 𝑣).
A labelled mixed graph is a mixed graph 𝑀equipped with a labeling function 𝜇: 𝑉(𝑀) →N. For 𝜚∈N,
we say that a labelled mixed graph (𝑀, 𝜇) is 𝜚-labelled if 𝜇(𝑣) ≤𝜚for every 𝑣∈𝑉(𝑀). As a shorthand,
we write 𝜇(𝑀) to denote the set 𝜇(𝑉(𝑀)) =

𝜇(𝑣) | 𝑣∈𝑉(𝑀)
	
.
Let (𝑀1, 𝜇1) and (𝑀2, 𝜇2) be two labelled mixed graphs such that 𝑉(𝑀1) ∩𝑉(𝑀2) = ∅. By the disjoint
union of (𝑀1, 𝜇1) and (𝑀2, 𝜇2), we mean the labelled mixed graph (𝑀, 𝜇) where 𝑉(𝑀) = 𝑉(𝑀1) ∪𝑉(𝑀2),
𝐸(𝑀) = 𝐸(𝑀1) ∪𝐸(𝑀2) and 𝐴(𝑀) = 𝐴(𝑀1) ∪𝐴(𝑀2), and the labeling function 𝜇: 𝑉(𝑀) →N is defined
as follows: for 𝑣∈𝑉(𝑀), we have 𝜇(𝑣) = 𝜇1(𝑣) if 𝑣∈𝑉(𝑀1), and 𝜇(𝑣) = 𝜇2(𝑣) if 𝑣∈𝑉(𝑀2).
Next, we introduce the cliquewidth of mixed graphs; the definition follows the standard definitions
w.r.t. both undirected and directed graphs, see e.g. [9] (see also the surveys of Courcelle and Engelfriet [8],
and of Dabrowski, Johnson and Paulusma [12]). Since mixed graphs contain both directed and undirected
edges, we introduce separate join operations for edges and arcs.
Definition 1.8 (Cliquewidth of Mixed Graphs). Consider the following operations on labelled graphs.
(1) Introduce operation: Create a vertex 𝑣with label 𝑖∈N. We denote this operation by intro𝑖(𝑣).
(2) Union operation: Disjoint union of two labelled mixed graphs (𝑀1, 𝜇1) and (𝑀2, 𝜇2) such that
𝜇1(𝑀1) ∩𝜇2(𝑀2) = ∅. We denote this operation by union((𝑀1, 𝜇1), (𝑀2, 𝜇2)).
(3) Relabeling operation: For a labelled mixed graph (𝑀, 𝜇), and for distinct 𝑖, 𝑗∈N, change the label of
all vertices with label 𝑖to 𝑗. We denote this operation by relabel𝑖,𝑗(𝑀, 𝜇).
(4) Edge-join operation: For a labelled mixed graph (𝑀, 𝜇), and for distinct 𝑖, 𝑗∈N, add an edge between
every vertex of label 𝑖and every vertex of label 𝑗. We denote this operation by e-join𝑖,𝑗(𝑀, 𝜇).
(5) Arc-join operation: For a labelled mixed graph (𝑀, 𝜇), and for distinct 𝑖, 𝑗∈N, add an arc directed
from every vertex of label 𝑖to every vertex of label 𝑗. We denote this operation by a-join𝑖,𝑗(𝑀, 𝜇).
A sequence of operations, in which each operation is one of the five operations above, is what we call
a clique-expression. For a clique-expression 𝜎, we will use (𝑀𝜎, 𝜇𝜎) to denote the labelled mixed graph
constructed by 𝜎. Let 𝜚be a positive integer. We say that a clique-expression 𝜎is a 𝜚-expression if at most
𝜚labels are used in the construction of (𝑀𝜎, 𝜇𝜎). Consider a mixed graph 𝑀. We say that 𝑀admits a
𝜚-expression if there is a 𝜚-expression 𝜎such that 𝑀= 𝑀𝜎; in this case, we also say that 𝜎is a 𝜚-expression
for 𝑀. The cliquewidth of 𝑀is the least integer 𝜚for which 𝑀admits a 𝜚-expression.
The toadwidth of a temporal pattern will be based on the cliquewidth of a mixed graph associated
with the pattern that takes into account both the underlying graph as well as the temporal constraints.

Moreover, 𝜗is the identity on {𝑎, 𝑐, 𝑑, 𝑒, 𝑓, ℎ} and maps the remaining edges to ⊥. (Right:) The order-augmented
dual 𝑀of 𝑃. Edges are depicted as solid lines and arcs are depicted as dash-dotted arrows. The toadwidth of 𝑃is
equal to the cliquewidth of 𝑀.
Definition 1.9 (Order-Augmented Duals). Let 𝑃= (𝐻, 𝑅, 𝜗) be a temporal pattern. The order-augmented
dual (“oad”) of 𝑃is a mixed graph 𝑀defined as follows:
𝑉(𝑀) = 𝐸(𝐻).
𝐸(𝑀) = {{𝑒, 𝑒′} ∈𝑉(𝑀)(2) | 𝑒≠𝑒′ ∧𝑒∩𝑒′ ≠∅}, that is, we add an edge between 𝑒and 𝑒′ if 𝑒∩𝑒′ ≠∅.
𝐴(𝑀) = {(𝑒, 𝑒′) ∈𝑉(𝑀)2 | 𝑒≠𝑒′ ∧𝜗(𝑒) ≤𝑅𝜗(𝑒′)}, that is, we add an arc from 𝑒to 𝑒′ if 𝜗(𝑒) ≤𝑅𝜗(𝑒′).
Consult Figure 3 for an example of the construction of an order-augmented dual.
We are finally able to formally define the toadwidth.
Definition 1.10 (Toadwidth). Let 𝑃= (𝐻, 𝑅, 𝜗) be a temporal pattern. The temporally order-augmented
dual width (the “toadwidth”) of 𝑃is defined as the cliquewidth of the order-augmented dual of 𝑃. We say
that a class 𝒞of temporal patterns has bounded toadwidth if there is a constant 𝐵such that the toadwidth
of each member of 𝒞is at most 𝐵.
The central algorithmic result of this paper states that counting homomorphisms from temporal
patterns of bounded toadwidth is fixed-parameter tractable. The restriction of the patterns is formalised
by specifying the class of allowed patterns in the problem definition as follows; note that this is standard
for counting problems in parameterised complexity theory [13, 10, 38].
Definition 1.11 (#TemporalHom(𝒞)). Let 𝒞be a class of temporal patterns. We define the following
parameterised counting problem.
#TemporalHom(𝒞)
Input: A temporal pattern 𝑃∈𝒞, and a temporal graph (𝐺, 𝜏)
Parameter: |𝑃|
Output: #Hom(𝑃→(𝐺, 𝜏))


## Page 9


8
Motif Counting in Temporal Graphs
We are now able to state our main algorithmic result. Note that we restrict ourselves to temporal
patterns without parallel edges for the sake of simplicity in our presentation, and we leave the case of
parallel edges for future work.
Theorem 1.12. Let 𝒞be a class of temporal patterns without parallel edges. If 𝒞has bounded toadwidth, then
#TemporalHom(𝒞) is fixed-parameter tractable.
Toadwidth vs. Treewidth
Readers familiar with parameterised counting might be aware that, in the static (non-temporal) setting,
the complexity of counting homomorphisms is determined by the treewidth of the pattern [13, 33]. We
argue that toadwidth is not just an arbitrary cliquewidth based width measure that happens to work
for the upper bound, but that toadwidth is a very natural measure arising in the context of temporal
constraints: First, it is well-known that a class of graphs has bounded treewidth if and only if the class of
the associated line graphs has bounded cliquewidth [22]. As a consequence, using the classification of
Dalmau and Jonsson [13], counting homomorphisms to non-temporal graphs is fixed-parameter tractable
if and only if the line graphs of the allowed patterns have bounded cliquewidth; the only-if direction
relies on standard lower bound assumptions in parameterised complexity theory. Now, in the temporal
setting, the order augmented dual is identical to the line graph of the pattern with added arcs for the
temporal constraints — as the temporal constraints involve edges, and not vertices, it is unsurprising
that we need to operate on the line graph. For this reason, we view our FPT algorithm in Theorem 1.12,
which operates via dynamic programming along toadwidth, as a natural temporal counterpart of the
well-established dynamic programming algorithm for counting static homomorphisms along treewidth.
In particular, we believe that toadwidth will contribute to the active search for finding an appropriate
temporal version of treewidth [19].
A Complexity Dichotomy for Totally Ordered Temporal Patterns
In the final part of the paper, we ask whether the fixed-parameter tractability result in Theorem 1.12
is optimal. While we do not answer this question in that generality, we analyse a modification of
#TemporalHom(𝒞) for which we only restrict the underlying graphs of the temporal patterns and for
which we consider only total orders. For this special case, formally introduced below, we establish an
exhaustive and explicit complexity dichotomy, the tractability criterion of which implies a bound on the
toadwidth.
In what follows, we say that a temporal pattern 𝑃= (𝐻, 𝑅, 𝜗) is totally ordered if 𝑅is a total order and 𝜗
is a bijection. To avoid notational clutter, we allow ourselves to write 𝑃= (𝐻, ≼), where ≼is a total order
on 𝐸(𝐻); moreover, we write 𝑒≺𝑒′ if 𝑒≼𝑒′ and 𝑒≠𝑒′.
Definition 1.13. Let ℋbe a class of graphs. We define the following parameterised counting problem.
#TemporalHomTO(ℋ)
Input: A totally ordered temporal pattern (𝐻, ≼) with 𝐻∈ℋ, and a temporal graph (𝐺, 𝜏)
Parameter: |(𝐻, ≼)|
Output: #Hom((𝐻, ≼) →(𝐺, 𝜏))
We are able to classify #TemporalHomTO(ℋ) completely along the semi-induced matching number of
the line graphs of ℋ:

J. Madathil, K. Meeks, and M. Roth
9
Definition 1.14 (Semi-Induced Matchings). A semi-induced matching of size 𝑘in a graph 𝐿is a set of 𝑘
pairwise disjoint edges {𝑢1, 𝑣1}, . . . , {𝑢𝑘, 𝑣𝑘} such that there are no edges in 𝐿between 𝑢𝑖and 𝑣𝑗for 𝑖≠𝑗.
The semi-induced matching number of 𝐿is the maximum size of a semi-induced matching in 𝐿, and we
say that a class of graphs has bounded semi-induced matching number if there is a constant 𝐵such that each
member of the class has semi-induced matching number at most 𝐵.
The third main result of this work reads as follows.
Theorem 1.15. Let ℋbe a recursively enumerable5 class of graphs. Assuming that FPT ≠W[1], the following
are equivalent:
#TemporalHomTO(ℋ) is fixed-parameter tractable.
the class of line graphs of ℋhas bounded semi-induced matching number.
The assumption FPT ≠W[1] is the standard hardness assumption in parameterised complexity theory
(see Section 2 for a concise introduction). While it is unknown whether FPT = W[1] implies P = NP, it is
known that FPT = W[1] refutes the Exponential Time Hypothesis [27, 6, 7], leading to subexponential
time algorithms for 3-SAT.
Finally, we note that the tractability part of Theorem 1.15 relies on our algorithm from Theorem 1.12,
and connects toadwidth with semi-induced matching number, via the following bound, which might be
of independent interest.
Lemma 1.16. Let 𝑏be a positive integer. Let 𝐻be a simple, undirected graph. If every semi-induced
matching in 𝐿(𝐻) has size at most 𝑏, then for every total order ≼on 𝐸(𝐻), the toadwidth of (𝐻, ≼) is at most
4𝑏4 + 12𝑏3 + 14𝑏2 + 6𝑏+ 2.

2.1
Graph Theory
Graphs in this work are undirected, and do not contain self-loops unless stated otherwise, but we allow
multi-edges for technical reasons — among others, this is to enable the presence of the same edge at
multiple times in a temporal graph. For an undirected graph 𝐻, we use 𝑉(𝐻) and 𝐸(𝐻) to denote the
vertex set and the edge set of 𝐻, respectively. A homomorphism from a graph 𝐻to a graph 𝐺is a pair
𝜑= (𝜈, 𝜉) where 𝜈: 𝑉(𝐻) →𝑉(𝐺) and 𝜉: 𝐸(𝐻) →𝐸(𝐺) such that for each 𝑢, 𝑣∈𝑉(𝐻) and for each
edge 𝑒with endpoints 𝑢and 𝑣, we have that 𝜈(𝑢) and 𝜈(𝑣) are the endpoints of 𝜉(𝑒).6 A homomorphism
5 We note that the restriction to recursively enumerable classes is standard in parameterised complexity theory as, without
this assumption, the parameter dependency in the running time of the reductions could not be bounded by a computable
function.
6 Note that, for simple graphs without multi-edges, a homomorphism is defined as just an edge-preserving mapping
𝜈: 𝑉(𝐻) →𝑉(𝐺), i.e., {𝑢, 𝑣} ∈𝐸(𝐻) implies {𝜈(𝑢), 𝜈(𝑣)} ∈𝐸(𝐺); this fully specifies the mapping 𝜉from 𝐸(𝐻) to 𝐸(𝐺).
However, for graphs with multi-edges, we need to specify 𝜉explicitly.

𝜑= (𝜈, 𝜉) is called an embedding if 𝜈and 𝜉are injective, and an embedding is an isomorphism if (𝜈−1, 𝜉−1)

The line graph of a graph 𝐺contains has vertex set 𝐸(𝐺), that is, every edge of 𝐺becomes a vertex, and

2.2
Temporal Graphs, Patterns, and Homomorphisms
Definition 2.1 (Temporal Graphs). A temporal graph is a pair Γ = (𝐺, 𝜏) of a graph 𝐺without selfloops,
but with parallel edges allowed, and a mapping 𝜏: 𝐸(𝐺) →N such that for every pair of parallel edges
𝑒≠𝑒′ we have 𝜏(𝑒) ≠𝜏(𝑒′).
As discussed in the introduction we will focus on the following type of isomorphisms between
temporal graphs, called order-isomorphisms.
Definition 2.2 (Order-Isomorphisms). Let Γ1 = (𝐺1, 𝜏1) and Γ2 = (𝐺2, 𝜏2) be temporal graphs. An
order-isomorphism from Γ1 to Γ2 is a pair (𝜋, 𝑡) of an isomorphism 𝜋= (𝜋𝜈, 𝜋𝜉) from 𝐺1 to 𝐺2 and a
bijection 𝑡: 𝜏1(𝐸(𝐺1)) →𝜏2(𝐸(𝐺2)) such that
(1) for all 𝑒∈𝐸(𝐺1) we have 𝑡(𝜏1(𝑒)) = 𝜏2(𝜋𝜉(𝑒)), and
(2) 𝑡is strongly monotonically increasing, i.e., 𝑥< 𝑦implies 𝑡(𝑥) < 𝑡(𝑦).
We write Γ1 o Γ2 if Γ1 and Γ2 are order-isomorphic.
Definition 2.3 (Temporal Pattern). A temporal pattern is a triple 𝑃= (𝐻, 𝑅, 𝜗) where 𝐻is a graph, 𝑅
is a finite poset, and 𝜗is a surjection from 𝐸(𝐻) →𝑅such that for ever pair 𝑒, 𝑒′ of parallel edges of 𝐻we
have 𝜗(𝑒) ≠𝜗(𝑒′).
To avoid notational clutter, given a temporal pattern 𝑃= (𝐻, 𝑅, 𝜑), we might interchangeably use 𝑅to
denote the poset as well as its groundset. Moreover, we use the symbols <𝑅and ≤𝑅for the relation of 𝑅,
that is, we write 𝑎≤𝑅𝑏if (𝑎, 𝑏) is a tuple of the partial ordering relation, and we write 𝑎<𝑅𝑏if 𝑎≤𝑅𝑏
and 𝑎≠𝑏.
Definition 2.4 (Homomorphisms from Temporal Patterns). Let 𝑃= (𝐻, 𝑅, 𝜗) be a temporal pattern,
and let Γ = (𝐺, 𝜏) be a temporal graph. A homomorphism from 𝑃to Γ is a homomorphism 𝜑= (𝜈, 𝜉)
from 𝐻to 𝐺such that for all 𝑒, 𝑒′ ∈𝐸(𝐻) we have 𝜗(𝑒) ≤𝑅𝜗(𝑒′) ⇒𝜏(𝜉(𝑒)) ≤𝜏(𝜉(𝑒′)) (implying that
𝜗(𝑒) = 𝜗(𝑒′) ⇒𝜏(𝜉(𝑒)) = 𝜏(𝜉(𝑒′))). We write #Hom(𝑃→Γ) for the set of all homomorphisms from 𝑃
to Γ.
Temporal Patterns with Total Orders
For our lower bounds, we will mainly rely on totally ordered temporal patterns, that is, 𝑃= (𝐻, 𝑅, 𝜗)
such that 𝑅is a total order and 𝜗is a bijection. To avoid notational clutter, we allow ourselves to write
𝑃= (𝐻, ≼), where ≼is a total order on 𝐸(𝐻); moreover, we write 𝑒≺𝑒′ if 𝑒≼𝑒′ and 𝑒≠𝑒′.
A homomorphism from a totally ordered temporal pattern (𝐻, ≼) to (𝐺, 𝜏) is then a homomorphism
𝜑= (𝜈, 𝜉) from 𝐻to 𝐺such that, for all 𝑒, 𝑒′ ∈𝐸(𝐻) we have 𝑒≺𝑒′ if and only if 𝜏(𝜉(𝑒)) < 𝜏(𝜉(𝑒′)).

2.3
Parameterised Complexity Theory
We provide a brief introduction to parameterised complexity theory and refer the reader to the standard
textbook of Flum and Grohe [18] for a detailed exposition (cf. [18, Chapter 14] for a treatment on
parameterised counting).
A parameterised counting problem is a pair (𝑃, 𝜅) of a function 𝑃: {0, 1}∗→Q and a polynomial-time
computable7 parameterisation 𝜅: {0, 1}∗→N. Parameterised decision problems are defined similarly, with
the only exception that the co-domain of 𝑃is {0, 1}. In what follows, we refer to both parameterised
decision and counting problems as parameterised problems.
A parameterised problem (𝑃, 𝜅) is fixed-parameter tractable (“FPT”) if there is a computable function 𝑓
and an algorithm A such that A, on input 𝑥, computes 𝑃(𝑥) in time
𝑓(𝜅(𝑥)) · |𝑥|𝑂(1) .
We call A an FPT algorithm for (𝑃, 𝜅).
A parameterised Turing-reduction from (𝑃1, 𝜅1) to (𝑃2, 𝜅2) is an FPT algorithm A for (𝑃1, 𝜅1) which
is given access to an oracle for 𝑃2 and which satisfies that, on input 𝑥, each oracle query 𝑦satisfies
𝜅2(𝑦) ≤𝑔(𝜅1(𝑥)), where 𝑔is a computable function independent of 𝑥. In other words, the parameter
of each oracle call depends only on the parameter of the input. We write (𝑃1, 𝜅1) ≤FPT
T
(𝑃2, 𝜅2) if a
parameterised Turing-reduction exists.
A parameterised problem (𝑃, 𝜅) is W[1]-hard if it reduces from the parameterised clique problem
Clique (defined below), that is Clique ≤FPT
T
(𝑃, 𝜅).

Parameter: |𝑃|

For the following problem definitions, we do not restrict the entire temporal pattern, but only the
underlying graph. Moreover, we also restrict to totally ordered “TO” patterns. We write ℋto denote a
recursively enumerable class of graphs.

3
Order-Isomorphisms and a Temporal Lov´asz Theorem
Our goal is to prove the following classification of temporal isomorphisms via homomorphism indistin-
guishability.
Theorem 3.1 (Theorem 1.6; restated). Two temporal graphs Γ1 and Γ2 are order-isomorphic if and only if for
all temporal patterns 𝑃we have #Hom(𝑃→Γ1) = #Hom(𝑃→Γ2).
We start with the easy direction.
Lemma 3.2. Let Γ1 = (𝐺1, 𝜏1) and Γ2 = (𝐺2, 𝜏2) and let 𝑃= (𝐻, 𝑅, 𝜗) be a temporal pattern. If Γ1 o Γ2
then #Hom(𝑃→Γ1) = #Hom(𝑃→Γ2).
Proof. Let (𝜋, 𝑡) with 𝜋= (𝜋𝜈, 𝜋𝜉) be an order-isomorphism from Γ1 to Γ2. There is a canonical mapping
𝑏from Hom(𝑃→Γ1) to Hom(𝑃→Γ2): Given 𝜑= (ˆ𝜈, ˆ𝜉) ∈Hom(𝑃→Γ1), set 𝑏(𝜑) := (𝜈′, 𝜉′) where
𝜈′(𝑣) = 𝜋𝜈(ˆ𝜈(𝑣)) and 𝜉′(𝑒) = 𝜋𝜉( ˆ𝜉(𝑒)), that is, we obtain the homomorphism 𝑏(𝜑) from 𝑃to Γ2 by
composing 𝜑with the order-isomorphism. This shows immediately that 𝑏(𝜑) is a homomorphism from
𝐻to 𝐺2. Now let 𝑒, 𝑒′ ∈𝐸(𝐻) such that 𝜗(𝑒) ≤𝑅𝜗(𝑒′). Since 𝜑is a homomorphism from 𝑃to Γ1, we have
that 𝜏1( ˆ𝜉(𝑒)) ≤𝜏1( ˆ𝜉(𝑒′)) and hence 𝑡(𝜏1( ˆ𝜉(𝑒))) ≤𝑡(𝜏1( ˆ𝜉(𝑒′))). Thus
𝜏2(𝜉′(𝑒)) = 𝜏2(𝜋𝜉( ˆ𝜉(𝑒))) = 𝑡(𝜏1( ˆ𝜉(𝑒))) ≤𝑡(𝜏1( ˆ𝜉(𝑒′))) = 𝜏2(𝜋𝜉( ˆ𝜉(𝑒))) = 𝜏2(𝜉′(𝑒′)) .
Next let 𝑒, 𝑒′ ∈𝐸(𝐻) such that 𝜗(𝑒) = 𝜗(𝑒′). Since 𝜑is a homomorphism from 𝑃to Γ1, we have that
𝜏1( ˆ𝜉(𝑒)) = 𝜏1( ˆ𝜉(𝑒′)) and hence 𝑡(𝜏1( ˆ𝜉(𝑒))) = 𝑡(𝜏1( ˆ𝜉(𝑒′))). Thus
𝜏2(𝜉′(𝑒)) = 𝜏2(𝜋𝜉( ˆ𝜉(𝑒))) = 𝑡(𝜏1( ˆ𝜉(𝑒))) = 𝑡(𝜏1( ˆ𝜉(𝑒′))) = 𝜏2(𝜋𝜉( ˆ𝜉(𝑒))) = 𝜏2(𝜉′(𝑒′)) .
Hence 𝑏(𝜑) is indeed a homomorphism from 𝑃to Γ2.
Symmetrically, we define 𝑏′ from Hom(𝑃→Γ2) to Hom(𝑃→Γ1), using the inverse of (𝜋, 𝑡). It is
straightforward to check that 𝑏◦𝑏′ and 𝑏′ ◦𝑏are the identity functions on the respective homomorphism
sets. Hence 𝑏is a bijection and the claim follows.
For the second, more interesting direction, we consider strict homomorphisms in an intermediate step.
Definition 3.3 (Strict Homomorphisms from Temporal Patterns). Let 𝑃= (𝐻, 𝑅, 𝜗) be a temporal
pattern, and let Γ = (𝐺, 𝜏) be a temporal graph. A homomorphism 𝜑= (𝜈, 𝜉) from 𝑃to Γ is called strict
if 𝜗(𝑒) <𝑅𝜗(𝑒′) ⇒𝜏(𝜉(𝑒)) < 𝜏(𝜉(𝑒′)). We write #Hom<(𝑃→Γ) for the set of all strict homomorphisms
from 𝑃to Γ.

Next, we need to introduce quotients of temporal graphs. To this end we first recall the definition
of quotients of static graphs with parallel edges: given 𝐻and a partition 𝜌of 𝑉(𝐻), the quotient 𝐻/𝜌
contains one vertex for each block 𝐵∈𝜌. Furthermore, for each edge {𝑢, 𝑣} ∈𝐸(𝐻), we create an edge
{𝐵𝑢, 𝐵𝑣} in 𝐻/𝜌, where 𝐵𝑢and 𝐵𝑣denote, respectively, the blocks of 𝜌that contain 𝑢and 𝑣. Note that
𝐵𝑢= 𝐵𝑣is possible - in that case, we create a self-loop.8
Definition 3.5 (Quotients of Temporal Patterns). Let 𝑃= (𝐻, 𝑅, 𝜗) be a temporal pattern, and let 𝜌be
a partition of 𝑉(𝐻). The quotient 𝑃/𝜌is a temporal pattern defined has follows
(1) We construct the quotient graph 𝐻/𝜌, but we do not (yet) delete newly created parallel edges.
(2) While there is a pair of parallel edges 𝑒, 𝑒′ of 𝐻/𝜌such that 𝜗(𝑒) = 𝜗(𝑒′), we delete one of 𝑒or 𝑒′. The
resulting graph is denoted by (𝐻/𝜌)↓.
Then we set 𝑃/𝜌:= ((𝐻/𝜌)↓, 𝑅, 𝜗|𝐸((𝐻/𝜌)↓)).
8 While we technically allow self-loops for quotients, we note that quotients with self-loops will not influence our proofs as
temporal graphs do not have self-loops.

Lemma 3.7. Let 𝑃= (𝐻, 𝑅, 𝜗) be a temporal pattern, and let Γ = (𝐺, 𝜏) be a temporal graph.
Let

and thus 𝜉(𝑒) ≠𝜉(𝑒′) which yields a contradiction.

4
An FPT Algorithm for Temporal Patterns of Bounded Toadwidth
In this section, we prove our main algorithmic result, i.e., Theorem 1.12, which says that #TemporalHom(𝒞)
is fixed-parameter tractable if 𝒞has bounded toadwidth. To do this, we design an involved dynamic
programming algorithm along toadwidth.
The remainder of this section is dedicated to designing this algorithm, and we assume throughout this
part that temporal patterns do not have parallel edges. We begin by introducing notation and proving a
number of preparatory results.
4.1
Further Notation and Terminology
For 𝐸′ ⊆𝐸(𝐻), we use 𝑉(𝐸′) to denote the set of vertices of 𝐻that are the endpoints of the edges in 𝐸′; that
is, 𝑉(𝐸′) = {𝑣∈𝑉(𝐻) | there exists 𝑒∈𝐸′ such that 𝑒is incident with 𝑣}. For 𝐸′ ⊆𝐸(𝐻), the subgraph
of 𝐻induced by 𝐸′, denoted by 𝐻[𝐸′], is the subgraph of 𝐻whose vertex set is 𝑉(𝐸′) and edge set is
𝐸′. For 𝑉′ ⊆𝑉(𝐻), we use 𝐸(𝑉′) to denote the set of edges that are incident with a vertex in 𝑉′; that
is, 𝐸(𝑉′) = {𝑒∈𝐸(𝐻) | there exist 𝑣∈𝑉′ and 𝑢∈𝑉(𝐻) such that 𝑒= 𝑢𝑣}. For a graph 𝐻, we say that
𝑋⊆𝑉(𝐻) is an independent vertex cover of 𝐻if 𝑋is an independent set and a vertex cover of 𝐻.
Viable graphs
We say that a simple, undirected graph 𝐻is a star if 𝐻has no isolated vertices, and there exists a vertex
𝑤∈𝑉(𝐻) such that every edge in 𝐻is incident with 𝑤; we call such a vertex 𝑤a centre of 𝐻. Notice that
if 𝐻is a star with at least two edges, then 𝐻has a unique centre, and otherwise 𝐻consists of a single
edge, say edge 𝑒= 𝑤1𝑤2, and both 𝑤1 and 𝑤2 are centres of 𝐻.
Let 𝐻be a simple, undirected graph. We say that 𝐻is viable if 𝐻has no isolated vertices, and 𝐻has
an independent vertex cover of size at most 2. We say that 𝐻is non-viable if 𝐻has no isolated vertices
and 𝐻is not viable. We now show that a viable graph 𝐻has a unique independent vertex cover of size
at most 2, unless 𝐻has at most two edges, or 𝐻is a path or a cycle with exactly 4 vertices, or 𝐻has a
connected component consisting of a single edge. See Figures 4 and 5.
Lemma 4.1. Let 𝐻be a viable graph. Then 𝐻is covered by one of the following four cases:
(Viable-1). 𝐻has at most 2 edges;
(Viable-2). 𝐻is a path or a cycle on exactly 4 vertices;
(Viable-3). 𝐻has a connected component with a single edge;
(Viable-4). 𝐻has a unique independent vertex cover of size at most 2.
Proof. First, since 𝐻is viable, 𝐻has no isolated vertices, and therefore 𝐻contains at least one edge,
and consequently, every vertex cover of 𝐻must have size at least 1. Suppose first that 𝐻has a vertex
cover of size exactly 1, i.e., 𝐻is a star. Now, if 𝐻has at most 2 edges, then 𝐻is covered by case Viable-1.
Otherwise, 𝐻is a star with at least 3 edges, and therefore 𝐻has a unique centre, say 𝑤∈𝑉(𝐻); then {𝑤}
is the unique independent vertex cover of size at most 2.
So assume from now on that 𝐻has no vertex cover of size exactly 1. Since 𝐻is viable, 𝐻has an
independent vertex cover of size at most 2; let {𝑤1, 𝑤2} ⊆𝑉(𝐻) be such a vertex cover. As 𝐻has no
isolated vertices, every vertex of 𝑉(𝐻) \ {𝑤1, 𝑤2} is adjacent to at least one (and possibly both) of 𝑤1
and 𝑤2. Notice also that as 𝑉(𝐻) \ {𝑤1, 𝑤2} is an independent set, for every vertex 𝑥∈𝑉(𝐻) \ {𝑤1, 𝑤2},
we have 𝑁(𝑥) ⊆{𝑤1, 𝑤2}; in particular, we have 𝑁(𝑥) = {𝑤1} or 𝑁(𝑥) = {𝑤2} or 𝑁(𝑥) = {𝑤1, 𝑤2}. For
𝑖∈{1, 2}, let 𝑁𝑖be the set of all vertices 𝑥in 𝑉(𝐻) \ {𝑤1, 𝑤2} with 𝑁(𝑥) = {𝑤𝑖}, and let 𝑁12 be the set of

Centre-set of a viable graph.
Consider a viable graph 𝐻. The centre-set of 𝐻, denoted by 𝑊(𝐻), is the
set of all vertices in 𝐻that belong to an independent vertex cover of size at most 2; that is,
𝑊(𝐻) =

𝑤∈𝑉(𝐻)
 there exists 𝑋⊆𝑉(𝐻) such that 𝑋is an independent vertex cover of 𝐻,
|𝑋| ≤2, and 𝑤∈𝑋

.
Naturally, if 𝐻has a unique independent vertex cover of size at most 2, then the centre-set is precisely this
independent vertex cover. However, there is some variability for the corner cases (Viable-1 to Viable-4) of
the previous lemma. For technical reasons, we provide explicitly the properties of the centre-sets in the
following result.
Corollary 4.2. Consider a viable graph 𝐻. Then |𝑊(𝐻)| ≤4. In particular, the following assertions are true.
Case 1. If 𝐻has at most two edges, then |𝑉(𝐻)| ≤4 and 𝑊(𝐻) = 𝑉(𝐻).
Case 2. If 𝐻is a path or a cycle on exactly 4 vertices, then 𝑊(𝐻) = 𝑉(𝐻).
Case 3. If 𝐻has at least 3 edges, and 𝐻has a connected component 𝐶with a single edge 𝑒= 𝑢𝑣, then 𝐻has
exactly one other component 𝐶′, and 𝐶′ is star with at least two edges. Moreover, 𝑊(𝐻) = {𝑢, 𝑣, 𝑤}, where 𝑤
is the unique centre of 𝐶′.
Case 4. Suppose 𝐻is a star (or equivalently if 𝐻has a vertex cover of size exactly 1) with at least 3 edges, then
𝑊(𝐻) is the singleton set consisting of the unique centre of 𝐻.
Case 5. If 𝐻is not covered by any of the three previous cases, then 𝐻has no vertex cover of size 1, and 𝐻has a
unique independent vertex cover 𝑋of size exactly 2, and consequently 𝑊(𝐻) = 𝑋.
Proof. Observe first that the five cases in the statement of the lemma together show that |𝑊(𝐻)| ≤4. Let
us now see that each of the cases is indeed true.
Suppose first that 𝐻has at most two edges; recall that as 𝐻is viable, 𝐻has no isolated vertices,
and hence 𝐻has either exactly one edge or exactly two edges. Suppose 𝐻has exactly one edge, say
𝐸(𝐻) = {𝑢𝑣}, then 𝑉(𝐻) = {𝑢, 𝑣} and both {𝑢} and {𝑣} are independent vertex covers of size at most 2, and


## Page 20


J. Madathil, K. Meeks, and M. Roth
19
hence 𝑊(𝐻) = 𝑉(𝐻) = {𝑢, 𝑣}. On the other hand, suppose 𝐻has exactly two edges, say 𝐸(𝐻) = {𝑒, 𝑒′}.
Then there are two cases: either 𝑒and 𝑒′ share an endpoint or they do not. In the former case, say
𝑒= 𝑢𝑣and 𝑒′ = 𝑢𝑣′, we have 𝑉(𝐻) = {𝑢, 𝑣, 𝑣′}, and the sets {𝑢} and {𝑣, 𝑣′} are both independent vertex
covers of size at most 2, and consequently 𝑊(𝐻) = {𝑢, 𝑣, 𝑣′}. In the latter case, say 𝑒= 𝑢𝑣and 𝑒′ = 𝑢′𝑣′,
where 𝑢, 𝑣, 𝑢′, 𝑣′ are all distinct, we have 𝑉(𝐻) = {𝑢, 𝑣, 𝑢′, 𝑣′}, and the sets {𝑢, 𝑢′} and {𝑣, 𝑣′} are both
independent vertex covers of size at most 2, and consequently 𝑊(𝐻) = {𝑢, 𝑣, 𝑢′, 𝑣′}.
If 𝐻is path on 4 vertices, say 𝑉(𝐻) = {𝑣1, 𝑣2, 𝑣3, 𝑣4} and 𝐸(𝐻) = {𝑣1𝑣2, 𝑣2𝑣3, 𝑣3𝑣4}, then notice
that both {𝑣1, 𝑣3} and {𝑣2, 𝑣4} are independent vertex covers of 𝐻, and consequently, 𝑣1, 𝑣2, 𝑣3, 𝑣4 ∈
𝑊(𝐻). Similarly, if 𝐻is a cycle on exactly 4 vertices, with say, 𝑉(𝐻) = {𝑤1, 𝑤2, 𝑤3, 𝑤4} and 𝐸(𝐻) =
{𝑤1𝑤2, 𝑤2𝑤3, 𝑤3𝑤4, 𝑤4𝑤1}, then again, {𝑤1, 𝑤3} and {𝑤2, 𝑤4} are independent vertex covers of 𝐻, and
consequently 𝑤1, 𝑤2, 𝑤3, 𝑤4 ∈𝑊(𝐻).
Suppose now that 𝐻has at least 3 edges, and 𝐻has a connected component 𝐶with a single edge
𝑒= 𝑢𝑣. First of all, observe that 𝐻has exactly two connected components. To see this, notice that as 𝐻
has no isolated vertex, each connected component of 𝐻contains an edge, and therefore every vertex cover
must contain at least one vertex from each connected component. And as 𝐻is viable and therefore has a
vertex cover of size at most 2, we can conclude that 𝐻has at most two connected components. But as 𝐻
has at least 3 edges, 𝐻must have a component 𝐶′ ≠𝐶with at least two edges. Thus 𝐶and 𝐶′ are the only
two connected components of 𝐻. And notice that every vertex cover of 𝐻of size 2 must contain exactly
one vertex from 𝐶and exactly one vertex from 𝐶′. Therefore 𝐶′ has a vertex cover of size exactly 1, or
equivalently 𝐶′ is a star. As 𝐶′ is a star with at least two edges, 𝐶′ has a unique centre 𝑤, and notice that
{𝑤} is the only vertex cover of 𝐶′ of size 1. Notice now that both {𝑢, 𝑤} and {𝑣, 𝑤} are independent vertex
covers of 𝐻, and they are the only independent vertex covers of 𝐻of size 2. Thus 𝑊(𝐻) = {𝑢, 𝑣, 𝑤}.
Suppose now that 𝐻is a star with at least 3 edges. Then 𝐻has a unique centre; let 𝑤′ ∈𝑉(𝐻) be
the unique centre. First of all, {𝑤′} is an independent vertex cover of 𝐻of size at most 2, and hence
𝑤′ ∈𝑊(𝐻). Now, as 𝑤′ has at least 3 edges incident with it, we have |𝑁(𝑤′)| ≥3, and therefore, any vertex
cover not containing 𝑤′ must contain 𝑁(𝑤′), and consequently must have size at least 3. Thus {𝑤′} is the
only vertex cover of 𝐻of size at most 2. Hence 𝑊(𝐻) = {𝑤′}.
Suppose now that 𝐻is not covered by any of the three cases considered above. Since Cases 1, 2 and 3
do not apply, by Lemma 4.1, 𝐻has a unique independent vertex cover 𝑋of size at most 2. And since
Case 4, does not apply, 𝐻does not have a vertex cover of size 1, and therefore we can conclude that 𝐻
has a unique independent vertex cover 𝑋of size exactly 2. Then by the definition of 𝑊(𝐻), we have
𝑊(𝐻) = 𝑋.
Observation 1. Observe that a graph without isolated vertices and with at most two edges is viable. And recall
that by definition, a non-viable graph has no isolated vertices. Every non-viable graph, therefore, has at least three
edges.
4.1.1
Cliquewidth of a mixed graph
Definition 4.3 (Mixed graph). A mixed graph is a graph that may contain both undirected and directed
edges. For convenience, we call the undirected edges simply ”edges” and the directed edges ”arcs.” For a
mixed graph 𝑀, we use 𝐸(𝑀) to denote the set of edges of 𝑀, and 𝐴(𝑀) to denote the set of arcs of 𝑀.
For vertices 𝑢, 𝑣∈𝑉(𝑀), we denote the undirected edge between 𝑢and 𝑣by 𝑢𝑣or {𝑢, 𝑣}, and we denote
the arc directed from 𝑢to 𝑣by (𝑢, 𝑣).
By a labelled mixed graph, we mean a mixed graph 𝑀equipped with a labeling function 𝜇: 𝑉(𝑀) →N;
we may not always mention 𝜇explicitly and instead simply write that 𝑀is a labelled mixed graph. For


## Page 21


20
Motif Counting in Temporal Graphs
𝜚∈N, we say that a labelled mixed graph (𝑀, 𝜇) is 𝜚-labelled if 𝜇(𝑣) ≤𝜚for every 𝑣∈𝑉(𝑀). As a
shorthand, we write 𝜇(𝑀) to denote the set 𝜇(𝑉(𝑀)) =

𝜇(𝑣) | 𝑣∈𝑉(𝑀)
	
. Let (𝑀1, 𝜇1) and (𝑀2, 𝜇2) be
two labelled mixed graphs such that 𝑉(𝑀1) ∩𝑉(𝑀2) = ∅. By the disjoint union of (𝑀1, 𝜇1) and (𝑀2, 𝜇2),
we mean the labelled mixed graph (𝑀, 𝜇) where 𝑉(𝑀) = 𝑉(𝑀1) ∪𝑉(𝑀2), 𝐸(𝑀) = 𝐸(𝑀1) ∪𝐸(𝑀2) and
𝐴(𝑀) = 𝐴(𝑀1) ∪𝐴(𝑀2), and the labeling function 𝜇: 𝑉(𝑀) →N is defined as follows: for 𝑣∈𝑉(𝑀), we
have 𝜇(𝑣) = 𝜇1(𝑣) if 𝑣∈𝑉(𝑀1), and 𝜇(𝑣) = 𝜇2(𝑣) if 𝑣∈𝑉(𝑀2).
We consider the following five operations involving labelled graphs.
(1) Introduce operation: Create a vertex 𝑣with label 𝑖∈N. We denote this operation by intro𝑖(𝑣).
(2) Union operation: Disjoint union of two labelled mixed graphs (𝑀1, 𝜇1) and (𝑀2, 𝜇2) such that
𝜇1(𝑀1) ∩𝜇2(𝑀2) = ∅. We denote this operation by union((𝑀1, 𝜇1), (𝑀2, 𝜇2)).
(3) Relabeling operation: For a labelled mixed graph (𝑀, 𝜇), and for distinct 𝑖, 𝑗∈N, change the label of
all vertices with label 𝑖to 𝑗. We denote this operation by relabel𝑖,𝑗(𝑀, 𝜇).
(4) Edge-join operation: For a labelled mixed graph (𝑀, 𝜇), and for distinct 𝑖, 𝑗∈N, add an edge between
every vertex of label 𝑖and every vertex of label 𝑗. We denote this operation by e-join𝑖,𝑗(𝑀, 𝜇).
(5) Arc-join operation: For a labelled mixed graph (𝑀, 𝜇), and for distinct 𝑖, 𝑗∈N, add an arc directed
from every vertex of label 𝑖to every vertex of label 𝑗. We denote this operation by a-join𝑖,𝑗(𝑀, 𝜇).
A sequence of operations, in which each operation is one of the five operations above, is what we call
a clique-expression. For a clique-expression 𝜎, we will use (𝑀𝜎, 𝜇𝜎) to denote the labelled mixed graph
constructed by 𝜎.
Consider a clique-expression 𝜎and the labelled mixed graph (𝑀𝜎, 𝜇𝜎) constructed by 𝜎. We can
associate 𝜎with a rooted binary tree 𝑇𝜎in a natural way: Each node 𝑧∈𝑉(𝑇𝜎) corresponds to an operation
in 𝜎, and in particular, each leaf of 𝑇𝜎corresponds to an introduce operation. Notice that each node of 𝑇𝜎
that corresponds to an edge-join, arc-join or a relabeling operation has exactly one child, whereas a node
corresponding to a disjoint union operation has exactly two children. For a node 𝑧of 𝑇𝜎, let 𝜎𝑧be the
sub-expression of 𝜎rooted at 𝑧, by which we mean the subsequence of 𝜎corresponding to the nodes in
the subtree rooted at 𝑧. Consider (𝑀𝜎𝑧, 𝜇𝜎𝑧), the labelled mixed graph constructed by 𝜎𝑧. Notice that 𝑀𝜎𝑧
is a subgraph of 𝑀consisting of all the vertices, edges and arcs introduced in the subtree rooted at 𝑧. In
particular, for the root ˆ𝑧of 𝑇𝜎, we have (𝑀𝜎ˆ𝑧, 𝜇𝜎ˆ𝑧) = (𝑀𝜎, 𝜇𝜎). For a node 𝑧∈𝑉(𝑇𝜎) and a label 𝑖∈[𝜚], let
𝑉(𝑀𝜎𝑧,𝑖) be the set of vertices of (𝑀𝜎𝑧, 𝜇𝜎𝑧) with label 𝑖, i.e., 𝑉(𝑀𝜎𝑧,𝑖) =

𝑣∈𝑉(𝑀𝜎𝑧) | 𝜇𝜎𝑧(𝑣) = 𝑖
	
.
Let 𝜚be a positive integer. We say that a clique-expression 𝜎is a 𝜚-expression if (𝑀𝜎𝑧, 𝜇𝜎𝑧) is 𝜚-labelled
for every node 𝑧∈𝑉(𝑇𝜎). Consider a mixed graph 𝑀. We say that 𝑀admits a 𝜚-expression if there is a
𝜚-expression 𝜎such that 𝑀= 𝑀𝜎; in this case, we also say that 𝜎is a 𝜚-expression for 𝑀. The cliquewidth
of 𝑀is the least integer 𝜚for which 𝑀admits a 𝜚-expression.
From now on, we will only work with a fixed 𝜚-expression 𝜎and its sub-expressions 𝜎𝑧for 𝑧∈𝑉(𝑇𝜎).
So to avoid clutter, we may omit 𝜎from the superscript in the notation introduced above, and simply
write (𝑀, 𝜇) for (𝑀𝜎, 𝜇𝜎), 𝑇for 𝑇𝜎, (𝑀𝑧, 𝜇𝑧) for (𝑀𝜎𝑧, 𝜇𝜎𝑧), and 𝑉(𝑀𝑧,𝑖) for 𝑉(𝑀𝜎𝑧,𝑖).
Remark 4.4 (The disjoint labels requirement in the union operation and assumptions about the other
operations in the definition of 𝜚-expression). We note that in the union((𝑀1, 𝜇1), (𝑀2, 𝜇2)) operation,
the requirement that the labels of 𝑀1 and 𝑀2 be disjoint, i.e., 𝜇1(𝑀1) ∩𝜇2(𝑀2) = ∅, is usually not
included in the standard definition of cliquewidth. But this requirement is harmless, as imposing
𝜇1(𝑀1) ∩𝜇2(𝑀2) = ∅would only increase the cliquewidth by a multiplicative factor of 2. We also make a
few implicit assumptions about the relabeling, edge-join and arc-join operations: We assume that each
execution of these operations is not redundant. So, for example, we perform a relabel𝑖,𝑗(·) operation only
if there already exist at least one vertex of label 𝑖and at least one vertex of label 𝑗. Thus for a 𝜚-expression

𝜎, if node 𝑧of 𝑇𝜎corresponds to the operation relabel𝑖,𝑗((𝑀, 𝜇)) and 𝑧′ is the unique child of 𝑧, then
𝑉(𝑀𝑧′,𝑖) ≠∅and 𝑉(𝑀𝑧′,𝑗) ≠∅. As for the two join operations, we preform them only if the edges/arcs
inserted by these operations are not already present in the mixed graph. That is, we perform the operation
e-join𝑖,𝑗(·) only if there does not exist any edge between a vertex of label 𝑖and a vertex of label 𝑗; similarly,
we perform the operation a-join𝑖,𝑗(·) only if there does not exist any arc from a vertex of label 𝑖to a vertex
of label 𝑗. We also assume that each edge-join (resp. arc-join) operation adds at least one edge (resp. arc) to
the mixed graph. Again, these assumptions can all be made without loss of generality as any 𝜚-expression
can be transformed in polynomial time into an equivalent one that satisfies all these requirements. These
assumptions have previously been used in the literature; see, for example [3, 25].
Remark 4.5 (Number of nodes in the tree 𝑇). Consider a 𝜚-expression for a graph 𝑀and the
corresponding tree 𝑇. We will rely on the known fact that the number of nodes in 𝑇is 𝒪(𝜚2 · |𝑀|) [9]; it is
not difficult to verify that the 𝜚-expression 𝜎contains |𝑀| introduce operations, at most |𝑀| −1 union
operations, and 𝒪(𝜚2 · |𝑀|) other operations.
Defining the order-augmented-dual of a temporal pattern
For an undirected graph 𝐻, we use 𝐿(𝐻) to denote the line graph of 𝐻, which is the intersection graph of
the edges of 𝐻; that is, the vertex set of 𝐿(𝐻) is precisely the edge set of 𝐻, i.e., 𝑉(𝐿(𝐻)) = 𝐸(𝐻), and for
vertices 𝑒, 𝑒′ ∈𝑉(𝐿(𝐻)), the line graph 𝐿(𝐻) contains the edge 𝑒𝑒′ if and only if the edges 𝑒and 𝑒′ share
an endpoint in 𝐻, i.e., 𝑒∩𝑒′ ≠∅. When talking about a graph 𝐻and its line graph 𝐿(𝐻), we may refer to
𝐻as the primal graph. Let 𝑃= (𝐻, 𝑅, 𝜗) be a temporal pattern. We define the order-augmented-dual of
𝑃, denoted by oad(𝑃), as follows. First of all, oad(𝑃) is a mixed graph, and oad(𝑃) is a supergraph of 𝐿(𝐻).
We have 𝑉(oad(𝑃)) = 𝑉(𝐿(𝐻)), and we have 𝐸(oad(𝑃)) = 𝐸(𝐿(𝐻)). In addition, oad(𝑃) contains arcs: For
distinct vertices 𝑒, 𝑒′ ∈𝑉(oad(𝑃)), oad(𝑃) contains the arc (𝑒, 𝑒′) if and only if 𝜗(𝑒) ≤𝑅𝜗(𝑒′) in 𝑃.
4.2
Algorithm for computing #Hom(𝑃→Γ)
The following lemma is the main technical contribution of this section.
Lemma 4.6. There is an algorithm that, given a temporal graph Γ = (𝐺, 𝜏), a temporal pattern 𝑃= (𝐻, 𝑅, 𝜗)
of toadwidth at most 𝜚, and a 𝜚-expression 𝜎and the corresponding tree 𝑇𝜎for the order-augmented-dual of 𝑃as
input, runs in time |𝑃|𝒪(1) · |Γ|𝒪(𝜚), and outputs #Hom(𝑃→Γ).
Notation
We use the following notation throughout this section.
Let Γ = (𝐺, 𝜏) be a temporal graph and
𝑃= (𝐻, 𝑅, 𝜗)) be a temporal pattern. Let Λ denote the lifetime of Γ, i.e., Λ = max𝑒∈𝐸(𝐺) 𝜏(𝑒). Let
𝑀= oad(𝑃), the order-augmented-dual of 𝑃, and let 𝜚be the cliquewidth of 𝑀; that is, 𝜚is the toadwidth
of 𝑃. Let 𝜎be a 𝜚-expression for 𝑀; in particular, let (𝑀, 𝜇) denote the labelled graph constructed by 𝜎.
Also, let 𝑇= 𝑇𝜎, the rooted tree corresponding to 𝜎, and let ˆ𝑧∈𝑉(𝑇) denote the root of 𝑇. We assume
that we are given 𝜎along with Γ and 𝑃. For a node 𝑧of 𝑇, recall that 𝑀𝑧is the subgraph of 𝑀consisting
of all the vertices, edges and arcs introduced in the subtree rooted at 𝑧, and for a label 𝑖∈[𝜚], recall that
𝑉(𝑀𝑧,𝑖) is the set of all vertices of 𝑀𝑧with label 𝑖. Recall also that the elements of 𝑉(𝑀𝑧,𝑖) are edges in

the primal graph 𝐻, i.e., 𝑉(𝑀𝑧,𝑖) ⊆𝐸(𝐻). Thus the subgraph of 𝐻induced by 𝑉(𝑀𝑧,𝑖) is well-defined.9
We denote this subgraph by 𝐻𝑧,𝑖. In particular, 𝑉(𝐻𝑧,𝑖) is the set of all vertices of 𝐻that are the endpoints
of the primal-graph-edges in 𝑉(𝑀𝑧,𝑖), or more formally, 𝑉(𝐻𝑧,𝑖) ⊆𝑉(𝐻) is the set of all vertices 𝑣∈𝑉(𝐻)
such that 𝑢𝑣∈𝑉(𝑀𝑧,𝑖) for some 𝑢∈𝑉(𝐻). We also define 𝐻𝑧to be the union of 𝐻𝑧,𝑖s; that is, 𝐻𝑧is the
subgraph of 𝐻with 𝑉(𝐻𝑧) = Ð
𝑖∈[𝜚] 𝑉(𝐻𝑧,𝑖) and 𝐸(𝐻𝑧) = Ð
𝑖∈[𝜚] 𝐸(𝐻𝑧,𝑖) = 𝑉(𝑀𝑧). Similarly, we define 𝑃𝑧
to be the temporal pattern (𝐻𝑧, 𝑅𝑧, 𝜗𝑧), where 𝑅𝑧is sub-poset of 𝑅whose ground-set is precisely the
set 𝜗(𝐸(𝐻𝑧)) = {𝑡∈𝑅| there exists 𝑒∈𝐸(𝐻𝑧) such that 𝜗(𝑒) = 𝑡}, and the partial order ≤𝑅𝑧is simply the
restriction of ≤𝑅to 𝐸(𝐻𝑧), and similarly 𝜗𝑧is the restriction of 𝜗to 𝐸(𝐻𝑧). We may omit the superscript 𝑧
from 𝑅𝑧and 𝜗𝑧, unless it is absolutely necessary to specify them.
As 𝑉(𝑀𝑧,𝑖) is a set of vertices in oad(𝑃) = 𝑀and a set of edges in 𝐻, to avoid any confusion caused by
using the words vertices and edges, we will instead refer to the elements of 𝑉(𝑀𝑧,𝑖) as simply “elements.”
In what follows, we design a dynamic programming algorithm to compute #Hom(𝑃→Γ), which will
prove Lemma 4.6.
Outline of the DP
Given a temporal graph Γ, a temporal pattern 𝑃, a 𝜚-expression 𝜎for the order-augmented-dual of 𝑃and
the corresponding tree 𝑇, we do a DP over 𝑇as follows. At each node 𝑧of 𝑇, we count the number of
homomorphisms from 𝑃𝑧to Γ. To do this, we exploit the properties imposed by the line graph structure
of 𝑀. In particular, we will argue that for every “active” label class 𝑉(𝑀𝑧,𝑖), the corresponding subgraph
𝐻𝑧,𝑖is a viable graph; by an active label class 𝑉(𝑀𝑧,𝑖), we mean that all elements of 𝑉(𝑀𝑧,𝑖) may be
involved in a future e-join·,·(·) operation. That is, 𝑉(𝑀𝑧,𝑖) is active if there exists an ancestor 𝑧′ of 𝑧in
the tree 𝑇such that 𝑧′ corresponds to an e-join·,·(·) operation, and for every 𝑒∈𝑉(𝑀𝑧,𝑖), a subset of the
line-graph-edges incident with 𝑒is inserted at node 𝑧′. And if 𝐻𝑧,𝑖is not viable then the elements of
𝑉(𝑀𝑧,𝑖) cannot be involved in any future e-join·,·(·) operation. Using this observation, we define a small
subset of primal-graph-vertices 𝑊(𝑧) ⊆𝑉(𝐻𝑧) as follows: 𝑊(𝑧) contains all the vertices the centre-set
of 𝐻𝑧,𝑖for all active (and hence viable) 𝐻𝑧,𝑖. We will have |𝑊(𝑧)| ≤4𝜚. Now, to count the number of
homomorphisms, we guess how the vertices of 𝑊(𝑧) are mapped by a homomorphism. For each 𝑖∈[𝜚],
we also guess the earliest and the latest time-steps to which an edge of 𝐻𝑧,𝑖is mapped by a homomorphism.
And for each combination of these two guesses, we count the number of homomorphisms from 𝑃𝑧to Γ
that are consistent with the guesses.
Ingredients for the DP
Consider a node 𝑧of 𝑇and a label 𝑖∈[𝜚]. Recall that 𝐻𝑧,𝑖is the subgraph of 𝐻induced by 𝑉(𝑀𝑧,𝑖).
Notice that by definition, 𝐻𝑧,𝑖has no isolated vertex and hence |𝑉(𝐻𝑧,𝑖)| ≤2|𝐸(𝐻𝑧,𝑖)| = 2|𝑉(𝑀𝑧,𝑖)|. With a
slight abuse of terminology, we say that (𝑧, 𝑖) is viable if 𝐻𝑧,𝑖is viable, and that (𝑧, 𝑖) is non-viable if 𝐻𝑧,𝑖is
non-viable. Consider a viable pair (𝑧, 𝑖). Recall that 𝑊(𝐻𝑧,𝑖) denotes the centre-set of 𝐻𝑧,𝑖; by Lemma 4.2,
we have |𝑊(𝐻𝑧,𝑖)| ≤4.
The next lemma says that if (𝑧, 𝑖) is non-viable, then all the edges of 𝑀incident with 𝑉(𝑀𝑧,𝑖) have
already been inserted in the sub-tree rooted at 𝑧. Equivalently, no more edge-join operations involving
𝑉(𝑀𝑧,𝑖) can be performed at an ancestor of 𝑧.

Lemma 4.7. Let 𝑧∈𝑉(𝑇) and 𝑖∈[𝜚]. Consider the set 𝐹of edges of 𝑀incident with 𝑉(𝑀𝑧,𝑖), i.e.,

. If (𝑧, 𝑖) is non-viable, then 𝐹⊆𝐸(𝑀𝑧).
Proof. Assume that (𝑧, 𝑖) is non-viable, and assume for a contradiction that 𝐹⊈𝐸(𝑀𝑧). Let 𝑒𝑒′ ∈𝐹\𝐸(𝑀𝑧),
where 𝑒∈𝑉(𝑀𝑧,𝑖). Then the tree 𝑇must contain a node 𝑧′ at which the line-graph-edge 𝑒𝑒′ is inserted
through an edge-join operation. In particular, there is a node 𝑧′ ∈𝑉(𝑇) such that 𝑧′ is an ancestor of 𝑧, and
𝑧′ corresponds to the operation e-join𝑖′,𝑗′(·) for distinct 𝑖′, 𝑗′ ∈[𝜚] with 𝑒∈𝑉(𝑀𝑧′,𝑖′) and 𝑒′ ∈𝑉(𝑀𝑧′,𝑗′).
Notice that 𝑖′ need not be equal to 𝑖; in particular, on the unique path in 𝑇from 𝑧to 𝑧′, there could
be a sequence of relabel operations, say (relabel𝑖,𝑖1(·), relabel𝑖1,𝑖2(·), . . . , relabel𝑖𝑝−1,𝑖𝑝(·), relabel𝑖𝑝,𝑖′(·)
so that the elements of 𝑉(𝑀𝑧,𝑖) have the label 𝑖′ at node 𝑧′, and thus 𝑉(𝑀𝑧,𝑖) ⊆𝑉(𝑀𝑧′,𝑖′). Now, as 𝑧′
corresponds to e-join𝑖′,𝑗′(·), every element of 𝑉(𝑀𝑧′,𝑖′) is adjacent to every element of 𝑉(𝑀𝑧′,𝑗′). Then, as
𝑉(𝑀𝑧,𝑖) ⊆𝑉(𝑀𝑧′,𝑖′) and as 𝑒′ ∈𝑉(𝑀𝑧′,𝑗′), every element of 𝑉(𝑀𝑧,𝑖) is adjacent to 𝑒′.
Now, consider 𝐻𝑧,𝑖, the subgraph of 𝐻induced by 𝑉(𝑀𝑧,𝑖). We will show that there exists an
edge ˆ𝑒∈𝐸(𝐻𝑧,𝑖) such that ˆ𝑒𝑒′ ∉𝐸(𝑀), a contradiction to the earlier claim that every element of
𝑉(𝑀𝑧,𝑖) = 𝐸(𝐻𝑧,𝑖) is adjacent to 𝑒′; to do this, notice that we only need to show that there exists ˆ𝑒∈𝐸(𝐻𝑧,𝑖)
such that ˆ𝑒and 𝑒′ do not have any common endpoint. Let 𝑒′ = 𝑢′𝑣′. If neither 𝑢′ nor 𝑣′ are in 𝑉(𝐻𝑧,𝑖)
then no edge in 𝐸(𝐻𝑧,𝑖) has a common endpoint with 𝑒′, and therefore ˆ𝑒𝑒′ ∉𝐸(𝑀) for every ˆ𝑒∈𝐸(𝐻𝑧,𝑖);
notice that 𝐸(𝐻𝑧,𝑖) ≠∅as 𝐻𝑧,𝑖non-viable and hence has at least 3 edges, and therefore such an ˆ𝑒indeed
exists. So assume from here on that either 𝑢′ ∈𝑉(𝐻𝑧,𝑖) or 𝑣′ ∈𝑉(𝐻𝑧,𝑖) (or both). We now invoke the
fact that 𝐻𝑧,𝑖is non-viable, and in particular the fact that 𝐻𝑧,𝑖has at least 3 edges, but no independent
vertex cover of size at most 2. Suppose exactly one of 𝑢′ and 𝑣′ belongs to 𝑉(𝐻𝑧,𝑖), say 𝑢′ ∈𝑉(𝐻𝑧,𝑖) and
𝑣′ ∉𝑉(𝐻𝑧,𝑖). Notice then that there must exist an edge ˆ𝑒∈𝐸(𝐻𝑧,𝑖) such that ˆ𝑒is not incident with 𝑢′;
if not, then every edge in 𝐻𝑧,𝑖must be incident with 𝑢′, and hence 𝐻𝑧,𝑖would be a star with centre 𝑢′,
and consequently 𝐻𝑧,𝑖would be viable. Fix such an ˆ𝑒. As ˆ𝑒∈𝐸(𝐻𝑧,𝑖), both endpoints of ˆ𝑒belong to
𝑉(𝐻𝑧,𝑖), and by assumption 𝑣′ ∉𝑉(𝐻𝑧,𝑖); thus ˆ𝑒is not incident with 𝑣′ either, and therefore ˆ𝑒and 𝑒′ do
not have any common endpoint. Suppose now that both 𝑢′ and 𝑣′ belong to 𝑉(𝐻𝑧,𝑖). Notice that in this
case 𝑢′𝑣′ ∉𝐸(𝐻𝑧,𝑖), as 𝑢′𝑣′ = 𝑒′ ∉𝑉(𝑀𝑧,𝑖) = 𝐸(𝐻𝑧,𝑖). Thus {𝑢′, 𝑣′} is an independent set in 𝐻𝑧,𝑖, and as
𝐻𝑧,𝑖is non-viable, we can conclude that {𝑢′, 𝑣′} is not a vertex cover for 𝐻𝑧,𝑖, which implies that there
exists an edge ˆ𝑒∈𝐸(𝐻𝑧,𝑖) such that ˆ𝑒is not incident with 𝑢′ or 𝑣′. Thus ˆ𝑒and 𝑒′ do not have any common
endpoint in this case either. Therefore in either case, ˆ𝑒𝑒′ ∉𝐸(𝑀), a contradiction.
We have thus shown that the assumption 𝐹⊈𝐸(𝑀𝑧) leads to a contradiction, and hence the lemma
follows.
Lemma 4.7 tells us that if we must add line-graph-edges incident with 𝑉(𝑀𝑧,𝑖) at a node 𝑧′, which is

say 𝑣∈𝑉(𝐻). In this case, we can show that 𝑣is in the centre-set 𝑊(𝐻𝑧,𝑖).
Lemma 4.8. Let 𝑧∈𝑉(𝑇) and 𝑖∈[𝜚], and let 𝑒= 𝑢𝑣∈𝑉(𝑀𝑧,𝑖). If there exists 𝑒′ = 𝑢′𝑣∈𝑉(𝑀) \ 𝑉(𝑀𝑧,𝑖)

endpoint 𝑣, we have 𝑒𝑒′ ∈𝐸(𝑀). Observe now that as 𝑒𝑒′ ∈𝐸(𝑀) \ 𝐸(𝑀𝑧), there exists an ancestor 𝑧′ of 𝑧,
such that 𝑧′ corresponds to the e-join·,·(·) operation at which the (line-graph-edge) 𝑒𝑒′ is inserted. Then,
as 𝑒∈𝑉(𝑀𝑧,𝑖), we can conclude that 𝑒′ is adjacent in 𝑀𝑧′ to every element of 𝑉(𝑀𝑧,𝑖); that is, for every
ˆ𝑒∈𝑉(𝑀𝑧,𝑖), we must have ˆ𝑒𝑒′ ∈𝐸(𝑀), and therefore ˆ𝑒and 𝑒′ must have a common endpoint; we will use
this fact later in the proof.

by 𝑉(𝑀𝑧,𝑖).
Let us now argue that 𝑣∈𝑊(𝐻𝑧,𝑖). We will consider several cases, and invoke Lemma 4.2 to conclude
that 𝑣∈𝑊(𝐻𝑧,𝑖) in each case.
If 𝐻𝑧,𝑖has at most two edges, or if 𝐻𝑧,𝑖is a path or a cycle on exactly 4 vertices, then 𝑊(𝐻𝑧,𝑖) = 𝑉(𝐻𝑧,𝑖),

three edges. Suppose 𝐻𝑧,𝑖has a connected component 𝐶1 with a single edge. Then by Lemma 4.2-Case 3,
𝐻has exactly one other component 𝐶2, and 𝐶2 is a star with at least 2 edges. Let 𝑒1 be the single edge
in the component 𝐶1. Now, if 𝑒1 = 𝑒(= 𝑢𝑣), then by Lemma 4.2-Case 3, we have 𝑣∈𝑊(𝐻𝑧,𝑖). Suppose
this is not the case. Then 𝑒= 𝑢𝑣belongs to the component 𝐶2; we will argue that 𝑣is the unique centre
of 𝐶2, which again will imply that 𝑣∈𝑊(𝐻𝑧,𝑖). As 𝐶2 has at least two edges, there exists an edge 𝑒2 in
𝐶2 such that 𝑒2 ≠𝑒. As observed earlier, for every ˆ𝑒∈𝑉(𝑀𝑧,𝑖), and in particular, for ˆ𝑒∈{𝑒, 𝑒1, 𝑒2}, the
edges ˆ𝑒and 𝑒′ have a common endpoint; recall also that the common endpoint of 𝑒and 𝑒′ is 𝑣. We argue
that the only way this is possible is if the centre of 𝐶2 is precisely 𝑣, so that 𝑣is the common endpoint of
𝑒2, 𝑒and 𝑒′, (and 𝑢′ is an endpoint of 𝑒1). If 𝑣were not the centre of the star 𝐶2, then the centre must
be 𝑢, which implies that 𝑒2 = 𝑢𝑣2 for some vertex 𝑣2 ∈𝑉(𝐻𝑧,𝑖). Then, as 𝑒2 = 𝑢𝑣2 and 𝑒′ = 𝑢′𝑣share an

in the component 𝐶2. But then 𝑒′ and 𝑒1 (the single edge in the component 𝐶1) do not have any common
endpoint, which is a contradiction. Again by Corollary 4.2-Case 3, we have 𝑣∈𝑊(𝐻𝑧,𝑖).
Suppose 𝐻𝑧,𝑖is a star. This case is similar to the previous one; here also, it must be be the case that 𝑣is
the unique centre of 𝐻𝑧,𝑖. To see this, notice that as 𝐻𝑧,𝑖has at least 3 edges, there exist two distinct edges

In light of Lemma 4.10, we can define an equivalence relation ∼𝑧on the set Hom(𝑃𝑧→Γ) of all

Corollary 4.11. Let 𝑧∈𝑉(𝑇). Then Hom(𝑃𝑧→Γ) = Ò
(𝛼,t) comhom(𝑧, 𝛼, t), and #Hom(𝑃𝑧→Γ) =
Í
(𝛼,t) |comhom(𝑧, 𝛼, t)|, where both the union and the summation are over all pairs (𝛼, t) ∈maps(𝑧) × times(𝑧).
Corollary 4.11 tells us that to compute #Hom(𝑃𝑧→Γ), it is enough to compute |comhom(𝑧, 𝛼, t)| for
every (𝛼, t). In particular, we can compute #Hom(𝑃→Γ) by computing |comhom(ˆ𝑧, 𝛼, t)| for every
(𝛼, t) ∈maps(ˆ𝑧) × times(ˆ𝑧), where ˆ𝑧is the root of the tree 𝑇(and hence 𝑃ˆ𝑧= 𝑃). We now describe our
dynamic programming algorithm, which will do precisely this.
Definition of the states of the DP
We are now ready to define the states of our DP. For every node 𝑧of 𝑇, every map 𝛼∈maps(𝑧), and every
tuple t ∈times(𝑧) we define
DP[𝑧, 𝛼, t] = |comhom(𝑧, 𝛼, t)|.
Computation of the DP table entries
We compute the entries of the DP table in a bottom-up fashion over 𝑇. For each node 𝑧of 𝑇, assuming
we have computed all entries corresponding to all descendants 𝑧′ of 𝑧, we can compute the entries
corresponding to 𝑧.
We split the computation into cases depending on what kind of operation 𝑧
corresponds to.
Consider a node 𝑧∈𝑉(𝑇), a map 𝛼∈maps(𝑧) and a tuple t = (ˆ𝑡𝑞, ˜𝑡𝑞)𝑞∈[𝜚] ∈times(𝑧). We discuss below
how we can compute DP[𝑧, 𝛼, t].

Edge-join operation.
Suppose 𝑧corresponds to the operation e-join𝑖,𝑗(·) for distinct 𝑖, 𝑗∈[𝜚], and let 𝑧′
be the unique child of 𝑧. Notice that 𝑃𝑧= 𝑃𝑧′; that is, the addition of the line-graph-edges through the
e-join𝑖,𝑗(·) operation does not change the pattern. Notice also that 𝑉(𝑀𝑧,𝑞) = 𝑉(𝑀𝑧′,𝑞), and therefore
𝐻𝑧,𝑞= 𝐻𝑧′,𝑞for every 𝑞∈[𝜚]. We thus have 𝑊(𝑧) = 𝑊(𝑧′),
maps(𝑧) = maps(𝑧′),
times(𝑧) = times(𝑧′),
and consequently comhom(𝑧, 𝛼, t) = comhom(𝑧′, 𝛼, t). Therefore,
DP[𝑧, 𝛼, t] = DP[𝑧′, 𝛼, t].
We note that while it may seem at this point that the insertion of line-graph-edges through the e-join𝑖,𝑗(·)
operation does not matter for the computation of DP[𝑧, 𝛼, t], that is not the case. We used the existence of
line-graph-edges while computing the DP table entries corresponding to the union operation; specifically,
Claim 4.12 relies on the fact that appropriate line-graph-edges will be inserted at a future node.
Arc-join operation.
Suppose 𝑧corresponds to the operation a-join𝑖,𝑗(·) for distinct 𝑖, 𝑗∈[𝜚], and let
𝑧′ be the unique child of 𝑧. Notice first that 𝑉(𝑀𝑧,𝑞) = 𝑉(𝑀𝑧′,𝑞), and therefore 𝐻𝑧,𝑞= 𝐻𝑧′,𝑞for every
𝑞∈[𝜚]. We thus have 𝑊(𝑧) = 𝑊(𝑧′), maps(𝑧) = maps(𝑧′), times(𝑧) = times(𝑧′). Notice now that the only
difference between the patterns 𝑃𝑧and 𝑃𝑧′ is that 𝑃𝑧contains the additional constraints 𝜗(𝑒𝑖) ≤𝑅𝜗(𝑒𝑗) (or
more precisely 𝜗𝑧(𝑒𝑖) ≤𝑅𝑧𝜗𝑧(𝑒𝑗)) for every 𝑒𝑖∈𝑉(𝑀𝑧,𝑖) and 𝑒𝑗∈𝑉(𝑀𝑧,𝑗). Thus every (𝑧, 𝛼, t)-compatible
homomorphism is trivially (𝑧′, 𝛼, t)-compatible.
Now, consider an (𝑧′, 𝛼, t)-compatible homomorphism 𝜑= (𝜈, 𝜉). Let us see that 𝜑= (𝜈, 𝜉) is
(𝑧, 𝛼, t)-compatible if only if ˜𝑡𝑖≤ˆ𝑡𝑗. Suppose 𝜑= (𝜈, 𝜉) is (𝑧, 𝛼, t)-compatible. Then by condition (CC2)
Definition 4.9, there must exist ˜𝑒𝑖∈𝑉(𝑀𝑧,𝑖) with 𝜏(𝜉(˜𝑒𝑖)) = ˜𝑡𝑖, and there must exist ˆ𝑒𝑗∈𝑉(𝑀𝑧,𝑗) with
𝜏(𝜉(ˆ𝑒𝑗)) = ˆ𝑡𝑗. And as 𝜗(𝑒𝑖) ≤𝑅ˆ𝜗(𝑒𝑗), by condition (CC3), we have 𝜏(𝜉(˜𝑒𝑖)) ≤𝜏(𝜉(ˆ𝑒𝑗)), which implies that
˜𝑡𝑖= 𝜏(𝜉(˜𝑒𝑖)) ≤𝜏(𝜉(ˆ𝑒𝑗)) = ˆ𝑡𝑗. Conversely, suppose that ˜𝑡𝑖≤ˆ𝑡𝑗. To show that 𝜑= (𝜈, 𝜉) is (𝑧, 𝛼, t)-compatible,
notice that we only need to show that 𝜏(𝜉(𝑒𝑖)) ≤𝜏(𝜉(𝑒𝑗)) for every 𝑒𝑖∈𝑉(𝑀𝑧,𝑖) and 𝑒𝑗∈𝑉(𝑀𝑧,𝑗). By

This completes the description of the DP. We are now ready to complete the proof of Lemma 4.6.

5
A Classification for Totally Ordered Patterns
In this section we prove Theorem 1.15, which establishes a complete classification of the parameterised
complexity of #TemporalHomTO(ℋ), where ℋis a recursively enumerable class of static graphs. We
discuss the upper bound in Section 5.1, and the lower bound in Section 5.2. Throughout this section,
we only deal with totally ordered temporal patterns, i.e., patterns 𝑃= (𝐻, 𝑅, 𝜗) in which 𝑅is a totally
ordered set and 𝜗is a bijection. And we denote a totally ordered temporal pattern by (𝐻, ≼), where ≼is a
total order on 𝐸(𝐻).
5.1
Bounding Toadwidth via Semi-Induced Matching Number
In this section, we show that #TemporalHomTO(ℋ) is fixed-parameter tractable if the class of line graphs
of ℋhas bounded semi-induced matching number. To do this, we will prove that if the class of line graphs
of ℋhas bounded semi-induced matching number, then for every 𝐻∈ℋand every total order ≼on 𝐸(𝐻),
the toadwidth of the temporal pattern (𝐻, ≼) is bounded. The proof then follows from Theorem 1.12.
Let 𝐻be a simple, undirected graph, and let ≼be a total order on 𝐸(𝐻); thus (𝐻, ≼) is a temporal
pattern. Consider the line graph 𝐿(𝐻) of 𝐻, and the order-augmented-dual oad((𝐻, ≼)); from here on,
we will omit the second pair of parentheses, and simply write oad(𝐻, ≼) instead of oad((𝐻, ≼)). Let

Lemma 1.16. Let 𝑏be a positive integer. Let 𝐻be a simple, undirected graph. If every semi-induced

4𝑏4 + 12𝑏3 + 14𝑏2 + 6𝑏+ 2.
To prove Lemma 1.16, we prove the following stronger claim, which says that the toadwidth of (𝐻, ≼)

size, then every orsim in oad(𝐻, ≼) has bounded size for every total order ≼on 𝐸(𝐻). And therefore,
Lemma 5.1 does imply Lemma 1.16.

most 𝑏. Recall that the assumption that led to this contradiction was that the equivalence relation ∼𝑖
has at least 4𝑏4 + 12𝑏3 + 14𝑏2 + 6𝑏+ 2 equivalence classes. We can thus conclude that ∼𝑖has at most
4𝑏4 + 12𝑏3 + 14𝑏2 + 6𝑏+ 1 equivalence classes.
This completes the proof of the lemma.
5.2
Parameterised Intractability along large Semi-Induced Matchings
Recall that a semi-induced matching of a graph 𝐿is a set 𝑀of pairwise disjoint edges {𝑢1, 𝑣1}, . . . , {𝑢𝑘, 𝑣𝑘}
such that there are no edges in 𝐿between 𝑢𝑖and 𝑣𝑗for 𝑖≠𝑗. A 𝑃2-packing of a graph 𝐻is a set 𝑆of pairs
of 2-paths (𝑢1, 𝑣1, 𝑤1), . . . , (𝑢𝑘, 𝑣𝑘, 𝑤𝑘) such that the 𝑣𝑖are pairwise distinct.
Observation 3. Let 𝐻be a graph and let 𝐿= 𝐿(𝐻) be the line graph of 𝐻. If 𝐿has a semi-induced matching of
size 𝑘, then 𝐻contains a 𝑃2-packing of size 𝑘.
5.2.1
Construction of the Global Gadget
In this subsection, we will define and analyse the main construction employed in our hardness reduction.
To this end, we introduce an edge-coloured intermediate version of the homomorphism counting problem.
Definition 5.6 (𝐻-coloured temporal graphs). An 𝐻-colouring of a graph 𝐺is a homomorphism
𝑐= (𝜈𝑐, 𝜉𝑐) from 𝐺to 𝐻such that 𝜈is surjective.10 An 𝐻-coloured temporal graph is a triple (𝐺, 𝜏, 𝑐) such
that (𝐺, 𝜏) is a temporal graph, and 𝑐is an 𝐻-colouring of 𝐺.
Definition 5.7 (Edge-colourful homomorphisms). Let (𝐻, ≼) be a totally ordered temporal pattern,
and let (𝐺, 𝜏, 𝑐) be an 𝐻-coloured temporal graph with 𝑐= (𝜈𝑐, 𝜉𝑐). A homomorphism 𝜑= (𝜈𝜑, 𝜉𝜑) from
(𝐻, ≼) to (𝐺, 𝜏) is called edge-colourful if the mapping 𝑒↦→𝜉𝑐(𝜉𝜑(𝑒)) is a bijection on 𝐸(𝐻). In other words,
𝜑is called edge-colourful if it hits all edge-colours of 𝐺w.r.t. the edge-colouring 𝜉𝑐. We write
ColHom((𝐻, ≼) →(𝐺, 𝜏, 𝑐))
for the set of all edge-colourful homomorphisms from (𝐻, ≼) to (𝐺, 𝜏, 𝑐).
The majority of the work in this section is dedicated towards proving the following construction,
which will allow us to reduce the clique problem to counting colourful temporal homomorphisms from
totally ordered temporal patterns that contain large 𝑃2-packings.
Lemma 5.8. There is a polynomial-time algorithm A that receives as input
10 We note that the surjectivity condition is not usually present for 𝐻-colourings in the literature; however, for the purpose
of this work, we will rely on the surjectivity constraint.

a graph 𝐹with |𝑉(𝐹)| > 𝑘.

and only if #ColHom((𝐻, ≼) →(𝐺, 𝜏, 𝑐)) ≥1.

Lemma 5.16. Let ℋbe a recursively enumerable class of graphs. If the line graphs of ℋhave unbounded
semi-induced matching number, then #TemporalHomTO(ℋ) is W[1]-hard under parameterised Turing-reductions.

## Page 51


50
Motif Counting in Temporal Graphs
6
Conclusion and Future Work
We have provided the first comprehensive treatment of homomorphism counting in temporal graphs,
both with respect to descriptive complexity and expressive power in terms of a Lov´asz-style isomorphism
theorem, as well as with respect to algorithmic complexity in terms of an FPT dynamic programming
algorithm along the newly introduced toadwith of temporal patterns and the explicit complexity dichotomy
for counting homomorphisms from totally ordered temporal patterns.
While our results fully resolve the natural special case of total orders, subsuming e.g. an FPT algorithm
for counting temporal walks, the primary question left open for future work is whether the algorithm
along toadwidth is optimal even for not necessarily totally ordered patterns. In other words, and more
formally:
Is #TemporalHom(𝒞) intractable for each 𝒞of unbounded toadwidth?
We suspect the answer to the above question to be affirmative.
Moreover, we propose further work on isomorphisms and temporal homomorphism indistinguisha-
bility for temporal graphs: as illustrated in the introduction, there are various well-motivated notions
for isomorphisms of temporal graphs in the literature, and we believe it to be worthwhile to investigate
whether our proof for order-isomorphisms can be adapted for point-wise and time-wise isomorphisms
via modifying the definition of temporal homomorphisms.
Finally, with homomorphism counts being the basis for motif counting in static graphs [10], we believe
that temporal homomorphisms have the potential to provide a similar unifying framework for pattern
counting in temporal graphs. In fact, our proof of Theorem 1.6 already makes use of a transformation of
temporal subgraph isomorphisms to finite linear combinations of temporal homomorphisms, and we are
optimistic that similar transformations will allow us to relate the complexity of counting more complex
temporal motifs to the upper and lower bounds for counting temporal homomorphisms.
